Exodus — Saints Bible cross-references
Completed: Exodus 1:1–40:38. Book initial curated pass complete. Overall next: Ruth 1:1.
1165 completed source verses; 2259 completed cross-reference pairs.
Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.
1 target candidate(s) await source evidence or identity review and are excluded from completed pair totals; see source-checks.json.
Reference edition: Bespoke Saints English Translation. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Exodus 1:1
These are the names of the sons of Israel who entered Egypt with Jacob their father; each came with his whole household:
  Genesis 46:8 — Explicit migration recollection: Genesis introduces the names of Israel's sons entering Egypt with Jacob; Exodus resumes that same household migration with a corresponding introductory formula.
  Acts 7:14 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen describes Joseph summoning Jacob and his relatives into Egypt, supplying the family movement that Exodus's opening household list recalls.

Exodus 1:2
Reuben, Simeon, Levi, and Judah;
  Genesis 35:23 — Genealogical correspondence: Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah appear together among Leah's sons, identifying the four family heads named in the opening Egyptian household list.
  Genesis 46:8-12 — Explicit migration genealogy: The earlier migration catalogue identifies Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah and their family branches, supplying the Egyptian-entry genealogy summarized by these four names.

Exodus 1:3
Issachar, Zebulun, and Benjamin;
  Genesis 35:23-24 — Genealogical correspondence: Issachar and Zebulun are listed as Leah's sons and Benjamin as Rachel's son, identifying the three named brothers without imposing a different ordering on Exodus.
  Genesis 46:13-14 — Genealogical continuation: The migration catalogue records Issachar's and Zebulun's sons, supplying the household branches of the first two family heads named here.
  Genesis 46:21 — Genealogical continuation: The earlier migration catalogue supplies Benjamin's descendants, continuing the household branch of the third brother named here while retaining its own expanded family wording.

Exodus 1:4
Dan and Naphtali, Gad and Asher.
  Genesis 35:25-26 — Genealogical correspondence: Dan and Naphtali are Bilhah's sons, while Gad and Asher are Zilpah's, identifying the four brothers in Exodus without confusing their mothers.
  Genesis 46:16-18 — Genealogical continuation: The migration catalogue develops Gad's and Asher's family branches and identifies Zilpah's descendants, supplying household context for these two names.
  Genesis 46:23-25 — Genealogical continuation: Dan's and Naphtali's descendants appear in the earlier migration catalogue under Bilhah's family summary, supplying the branches of the other two brothers named here.

Exodus 1:5
Now Joseph was in Egypt; and all the souls descended from Jacob were seventy-five.
  Genesis 46:27 — Explicit migration-total correspondence: Genesis gives seventy-five for Jacob's Egyptian household and identifies Joseph's Egyptian-born descendants; Exodus retains seventy-five while noting Joseph's prior presence there.
  Acts 7:14 — Explicit retrospective total: Stephen likewise gives seventy-five relatives in Joseph's summons of Jacob's family, matching the supplied Exodus total without replacing it with another edition's count.
  Deuteronomy 10:22 — Related migration account with different totals: Deuteronomy recalls the fathers entering Egypt with seventy souls, whereas this Exodus text says seventy-five; the common migration setting is compared with both stated totals preserved.

Exodus 1:6
And Joseph died, and all his brothers, and all that generation.
  Genesis 50:26 — Explicit narrative continuation: Genesis closes with Joseph's death and burial rites in Egypt; Exodus includes his death in its notice that the earlier family generation has passed away.
  Acts 7:15 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen summarizes Jacob's descent to Egypt and the deaths of Jacob and the fathers, corresponding to the passing of the ancestral generation narrated here.

Exodus 1:7
And the sons of Israel increased and multiplied, and became numerous and exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the land multiplied them.
  Genesis 46:3 — Promise and narrative development: God had promised to make Jacob a great nation in Egypt; the multiplication and strength of Israel's descendants develop that promise in the migration's setting.
  Psalms 104:24 — Explicit retrospective account: The psalm recounts God's greatly increasing His people and making them stronger than their enemies, recalling the population growth and strength narrated here.
  Acts 7:17 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen expressly recalls the people's growth and multiplication in Egypt as the time of the promise approached, summarizing the development stated here.

Exodus 1:8
And another king arose over Egypt, who did not know Joseph.
  Acts 7:18 — Explicit retrospective quotation: Stephen repeats the arising of another Egyptian king who did not know Joseph, retaining the distinctive transition that introduces Israel's oppression.
  Psalms 104:25 — Related retrospective transition: The psalm moves from Israel's increase to hostility and deceit against God's servants, corresponding to the changed Egyptian treatment following the new king's appearance without supplying his historical name.

Exodus 1:9
And he said to his nation, “Behold, the people of the sons of Israel are a great multitude, and are stronger than we are.
  Exodus 1:7 — Same-chapter narrated growth and royal response: The narrator has described Israel's great increase and strength; the king now presents that population as stronger than his own people, keeping his comparative claim within his speech.
  Psalms 104:24 — Explicit retrospective population theme: The psalm's account of God's people becoming stronger than their enemies corresponds to the strength the Egyptian king invokes as grounds for alarm.

Exodus 1:10
Come, therefore, let us deal craftily with them, lest they multiply; and when war befalls us, they also join our adversaries, wage war against us, and leave the land.”
  Psalms 104:25 — Explicit retrospective hostility: The psalm describes hatred of God's people and deceitful treatment of His servants, corresponding to the king's proposed crafty policy against Israel.
  Acts 7:19 — Explicit retrospective account: Stephen recounts the king's crafty mistreatment and exposure of infants, summarizing the policy whose proposed purpose here is to prevent Israel's further growth.

Exodus 1:11
He set overseers of labour over them to afflict them with their work. They built fortified cities for Pharaoh: Pithom, Rameses, and On, which is the City of the Sun.
  Exodus 5:6-14 — Developed forced-labour account: The later taskmaster and brick-quota narrative develops the coercive work regime introduced here, while its straw restrictions are not retroactively inserted into this first construction notice.
  Deuteronomy 26:6 — Explicit retrospective oppression: The later confession says the Egyptians afflicted, humbled and imposed hard labours on Israel, recalling the overseers and afflicting work described here.

Exodus 1:12
But the more they humbled them, the more they multiplied and grew exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the Egyptians loathed the sons of Israel.
  Exodus 1:7 — Same-chapter intensified population repetition: Israel's increase and exceptional strength repeat the earlier growth notice, now expressly continuing despite Egyptian humiliation rather than ceasing under it.
  Psalms 104:24-25 — Explicit retrospective growth-and-hostility account: The psalm joins Israel's increase and strength with Egyptian hatred and deceit, corresponding to the conjunction of continued growth and loathing narrated here.

Exodus 1:13
The Egyptians oppressed the sons of Israel by force.
  Deuteronomy 26:6 — Explicit retrospective oppression: The later confession's hard labours and Egyptian humiliation recall the forcible oppression that this verse attributes to the Egyptians.
  Exodus 6:5 — Oppression and divine response: God later speaks of hearing the groaning of the Israelites whom Egypt enslaves, identifying the oppressive condition to which the renewed deliverance speech responds.

Exodus 1:14
They made their lives painful with hard labour in clay and brickmaking, and with all the work in the plains, in every task in which they enslaved them by force.
  Exodus 5:7-9 — Developed brick-labour account: The later refusal of straw, unchanged brick quotas and heavier tasks develop the brickmaking burden introduced here without making every task identical in both passages.
  Deuteronomy 26:6 — Explicit retrospective labour account: The later historical confession recalls Egyptian affliction and hard labours, summarizing the painful clay, brick and field work described here.

Exodus 1:15
The king of the Egyptians spoke to the midwives of the Hebrews, one named Sepphora and the other Puah.
  Exodus 1:16-19 — Same-chapter named participants and ensuing exchange: The following command and questioning concern the midwives introduced here, identifying the women who resist the male-child order and answer the king; no identity with Moses' later wife is inferred from similar names.

Exodus 1:16
And he said, “When you attend the Hebrew women in childbirth, and they are about to give birth, if the child is male, kill him; but if the child is female, preserve her.”
  Exodus 1:22 — Same-chapter expanded killing order: The later command extends the male-child killing policy to all Pharaoh's people and specifies the river, preserving the distinct midwife instruction and wider decree.
  Acts 7:19 — Explicit retrospective persecution: Stephen recalls the king forcing exposure of infants so they would not survive, summarizing the lethal treatment of children developed in these royal orders.

Exodus 1:17
But the midwives feared God. They did not do as the king of Egypt had commanded them, and they kept the boys alive.
  Exodus 1:20-21 — Same-chapter fear of God and ensuing treatment: The account next reports God's good treatment of the midwives and repeats their fear of God; the house-making clause retains its supplied agency rather than being rewritten as God building their houses.
  Acts 5:29 — Related obedience principle: The apostles' declaration that God must be obeyed rather than humans provides a later expression of the priority reflected in the midwives' fear of God and refusal of the king's killing command.

Exodus 1:18
And the king of Egypt called the midwives and said to them, “Why have you done this thing, and kept the male children alive?”
  Exodus 1:17 — Same-chapter action and royal questioning: The king's accusation that boys have been kept alive responds to the midwives' previously narrated refusal to obey him.
  Exodus 1:19 — Same-chapter question and reported answer: The midwives' childbirth explanation answers the king's question about the surviving boys; their statement is retained as their reported account rather than independently verified obstetric information.

Exodus 1:19
The midwives said to Pharaoh, “The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women, for they give birth before the midwives come to them.” And they bore children.
  Exodus 1:18 — Same-chapter answer to royal interrogation: The claim about Hebrew women's giving birth before attendance is the midwives' response to the king's accusation that they preserved the male children.
  Exodus 1:20 — Same-chapter reported answer and narrative sequel: The narrator follows the midwives' answer with God's good treatment of them and continued population growth, without separately verifying every generalization in their childbirth claim.

Exodus 1:20
God dealt well with the midwives, and the people multiplied and became very strong.
  Exodus 1:7 — Same-chapter population repetition: The people's continued multiplication and strength repeat the opening growth notice, showing that the intervening royal policy has not stopped the narrated increase.
  Exodus 1:17 — Same-chapter conduct and ensuing treatment: God's good treatment is narrated after the midwives' fear of God and preservation of the boys, keeping the particular conduct in view without adding an unreported universal ruling about every statement they made.

Exodus 1:21
And because the midwives feared God, they made houses for themselves.
  Exodus 1:17 — Same-chapter repeated fear of God: The account repeats that the midwives feared God, recalling the reason associated with their refusal to kill; the supplied statement that they made houses for themselves remains intact.

Exodus 1:22
And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”
  Exodus 2:3 — Royal decree and endangered-child setting: The river decree supplies the danger surrounding the mother's placement of her male child in a protected basket in the river marsh; her action is not equated with carrying out the killing order.
  Acts 7:19 — Explicit retrospective persecution: Stephen's account of infants exposed so they would not be kept alive recalls the lethal royal policy, without importing a different decree's wording into Exodus.
  Hebrews 11:23 — Royal decree and parental resistance: Hebrews recalls Moses' parents hiding him without fearing the king's decree, providing a retrospective account of resistance to the danger established here.

Exodus 2:1
Now there was a man of the tribe of Levi, who took one of the daughters of Levi and had her as his wife.
  Exodus 6:20 — Explicit parental genealogy: The later genealogy names Amram and Jochebed as the parents of Aaron, Moses and Miriam, supplying the named family of the Levite couple introduced without names here.
  Numbers 26:59 — Explicit parental genealogy: Numbers identifies Jochebed with Levi and names Aaron, Moses and Miriam as her children by Amram, corresponding to the Levite parentage introduced here without independently reconstructing its more difficult ancestry clauses.

Exodus 2:2
She conceived and bore a male child. Seeing that he was beautiful, they hid him for three months.
  Acts 7:20 — Explicit retrospective birth account: Stephen recalls Moses' birth, beauty before God and three months in his father's house, matching the child and concealment period narrated here.
  Hebrews 11:23 — Explicit retrospective faith interpretation: Hebrews recalls the beautiful child hidden for three months by his parents and interprets their action as faith, retaining that later interpretation alongside Exodus's narration.

Exodus 2:3
When they could no longer hide him, his mother took a basket for him and coated it with bitumen-pitch. She put the child in it and placed it in the marsh beside the river.
  Acts 7:21 — Explicit retrospective exposure account: Stephen summarizes the exposure followed by Pharaoh's daughter's taking and rearing Moses, giving the later outcome of the mother's basket placement without repeating every material detail.
  Exodus 2:5 — Same-chapter placement and discovery: Pharaoh's daughter finds the basket in the marsh where the mother placed it, connecting the protective placement with the narrated discovery.

Exodus 2:4
His sister watched from a distance to learn what would happen to him.
  Exodus 2:7-8 — Same-chapter watching and intervention: The sister who watches the child's fate then offers to find a nurse and calls the mother, developing her particular role without assigning an unreported age or name.

Exodus 2:5
Pharaoh’s daughter came down to bathe at the river while her attendants walked beside it. She saw the basket in the marsh, sent her attendant, and had it brought to her.
  Exodus 2:3 — Same-chapter discovery and earlier placement: The mother's basket placement in the marsh explains the object and location encountered by Pharaoh's daughter and her attendant.
  Acts 7:21 — Explicit retrospective rescue account: Stephen's statement that Pharaoh's daughter took up and reared Moses condenses the discovery and eventual adoption that begin in this river scene.

Exodus 2:6
And she opened it, and saw a child weeping in the basket; and Pharaoh’s daughter had compassion on him, and said, “This is one of the Hebrew children.”
  Exodus 1:22 — Royal policy and compassionate response: Pharaoh had ordered Hebrew boys cast into the river, while his daughter recognizes this Hebrew child and has compassion; the contrast concerns the narrated responses rather than an invented private motive.
  Exodus 2:10 — Same-chapter compassion and adoption outcome: The child recognized as Hebrew and pitied here later becomes Pharaoh's daughter's son, carrying the rescue into an explicit adoption notice.

Exodus 2:7
And his sister said to Pharaoh’s daughter, “Do you want me to call a nursing woman from among the Hebrews, that she may nurse the child for you?”
  Exodus 2:4 — Same-chapter sister's role developed: The child's sister moves from watching at a distance to proposing a Hebrew nurse, continuing the intervention of the same unnamed family participant.
  Exodus 2:8-9 — Same-chapter proposal and implementation: The sister calls the child's mother, and Pharaoh's daughter commissions her to nurse him and promises wages, providing the particular outcome of the nursing proposal without inventing a narrated payment.

Exodus 2:8
Pharaoh’s daughter said to her, “Go.” The young woman went and called the child’s mother.
  Exodus 2:7 — Same-chapter permission and proposed action: Pharaoh's daughter's instruction to go authorizes the sister's preceding offer to obtain a Hebrew nursing woman.
  Exodus 2:9 — Same-chapter mother's arrival and commission: The mother called by the sister receives the nursing commission and takes the child, showing how her arrival leads to the child's care.

Exodus 2:9
And Pharaoh’s daughter said to her, “Keep this child for me, and nurse him for me, and I will give you your wages.” And the woman took the child and nursed him.
  Exodus 2:7-8 — Same-chapter nursing arrangement established: The commission to nurse follows the sister's offer and the calling of the child's mother, identifying the woman who receives the paid care arrangement.
  Exodus 2:10 — Same-chapter nursing and return: After taking and nursing the child for Pharaoh's daughter, the woman later brings him to her when he grows, completing the arrangement's narrated transfer.

Exodus 2:10
When the child grew, she brought him to Pharaoh’s daughter, and he became her son. She named him Moses, saying, “I took him up out of the water.”
  Acts 7:21 — Explicit retrospective adoption: Stephen expressly says Pharaoh's daughter brought Moses up as her own son, corresponding to the adoption stated here.
  Hebrews 11:24 — Adoptive status and later choice: Hebrews later describes grown Moses refusing the designation son of Pharaoh's daughter, recalling the royal family status established by this adoption without replacing the supplied naming explanation.

Exodus 2:11
During those many days, when Moses had grown, he went out to his brothers, the sons of Israel. Seeing their suffering, he saw an Egyptian man striking a Hebrew, one of his own brothers, the sons of Israel.
  Acts 7:23-24 — Explicit retrospective intervention: Stephen recalls Moses visiting his Israelite brothers at forty and defending one being wronged, expanding the adult visit and observed oppression narrated here.
  Hebrews 11:24-26 — Explicit retrospective interpretation: Hebrews interprets grown Moses' identification with God's afflicted people as a choice over Egyptian privilege, providing a later perspective on his approach to his suffering brothers.

Exodus 2:12
He looked this way and that, and, seeing no one, struck down the Egyptian and hid him in the sand.
  Acts 7:24-25 — Explicit retrospective action and interpretation: Stephen recounts the Egyptian's killing as Moses' defence of the oppressed man and adds Moses' expectation of recognition as a deliverer; the added interpretation remains Stephen's account.
  Exodus 2:14 — Same-chapter concealed deed becomes known: The later accusation names the Egyptian's killing, reversing Moses' attempt to act unseen and conceal the body in the sand.

Exodus 2:13
And he went out on the second day, and saw two Hebrew men fighting; and he said to the one who did wrong, “Why do you strike your neighbour?”
  Acts 7:26 — Explicit retrospective peace attempt: Stephen recounts the following day's conflict between brothers and Moses' attempted reconciliation, corresponding to his question to the man doing wrong.
  Exodus 2:14 — Same-chapter rebuke and rejection: The wrongdoer's ruler-and-judge retort answers Moses' question about striking a neighbour, showing the response to his intervention.

Exodus 2:14
But he said, “Who made you a ruler and judge over us? Do you want to kill me, as you killed the Egyptian yesterday?” And Moses was afraid, and said, “Has this matter become so plainly known?”
  Acts 7:27-28 — Explicit retrospective quotation: Stephen repeats the ruler-and-judge challenge and the reference to the Egyptian killed yesterday, closely recalling the rejection recorded here.
  Acts 7:35 — Explicit retrospective reversal: Stephen expressly contrasts this rejected Moses with the man God later sends as ruler and redeemer, using the earlier challenge within his account of the divine commission.

Exodus 2:15
Pharaoh heard about this and sought to kill Moses. Moses withdrew from Pharaoh’s presence and settled in the land of Midian. Having come to the land of Midian, he sat down by the well.
  Acts 7:29 — Explicit retrospective flight: Stephen recalls Moses' flight and sojourn in Midian, continuing the rejection-and-flight episode without using his summary to name every Egyptian threat.
  Exodus 4:19 — Threat and later return permission: The later instruction to return because those seeking Moses' life are dead answers the danger that initially caused him to withdraw from Pharaoh into Midian.

Exodus 2:16
The priest of Midian had seven daughters who tended the sheep of their father Jethro. They came and drew water until they filled the troughs to water the sheep of their father Jethro.
  Exodus 3:1 — Household and pastoral setting: The later call scene places Moses with the flock of Jethro, priest of Midian and his father-in-law, continuing the named priestly flock setting introduced through the daughters.
  Exodus 2:18-19 — Same-chapter daughters' return and reported rescue: The daughters return and report the well rescue to their father, whom that notice calls Reuel; the earlier Jethro wording and later Reuel wording are both retained without an added genealogical harmonization.

Exodus 2:17
The shepherds came and drove them away, but Moses rose and rescued them, drew water for them, and watered their sheep.
  Exodus 2:19 — Same-chapter explicit rescue recollection: The daughters' report repeats Moses' rescue from shepherds, drawing of water and watering the flock, preserving their description of him as an Egyptian man.
  Genesis 29:9-13 — Well-side family encounter parallel: Jacob waters Rachel's flock before being welcomed by her family; Moses likewise waters a family's flock at a well before the ensuing household reception, with his additional rescue action kept distinct.

Exodus 2:18
And they came to Reuel their father, and he said to them, “Why have you returned so quickly today?”
  Exodus 2:16 — Same-chapter family setting with distinct names: The earlier notice introduces the seven daughters and their priestly father Jethro; the return notice calls their father Reuel, retaining both supplied names rather than inventing an intermediate generation.
  Exodus 2:19 — Same-chapter question and reported answer: The daughters answer the question about their early return by describing the stranger's rescue and watering assistance.

Exodus 2:19
They said, “An Egyptian man rescued us from the shepherds. He drew water for us and watered our sheep.”
  Exodus 2:17 — Same-chapter explicit action recollection: The account has already narrated Moses' rescuing and watering; the daughters now describe those same actions in explaining their return.
  Exodus 2:20 — Same-chapter report and hospitality response: The father's instruction to call the helper for bread responds to the daughters' account of the man who rescued them and watered their sheep.

Exodus 2:20
And he said to his daughters, “And where is he? Why have you left the man behind? Call him, therefore, that he may eat bread.”
  Exodus 2:21 — Same-chapter invitation and household outcome: The invitation to bring the man for bread is followed by Moses settling with the household and receiving Zipporah as wife, advancing the reception into a stated family relationship.

Exodus 2:21
Moses settled with the man, who gave him his daughter Zipporah as his wife.
  Exodus 18:2-4 — Explicit later family identification: The later visit identifies Zipporah as Moses' wife and names their two sons, carrying forward the marriage introduced here.
  Acts 7:29 — Retrospective Midian family account: Stephen's summary says Moses became a stranger in Midian and fathered two sons there, supplying the family outcome of the settlement and marriage while not naming his wife in that summary.

Exodus 2:22
And the woman conceived and bore a son; and Moses called his name Gershom, saying, “I am a sojourner in a foreign land.”
  Exodus 18:3 — Explicit naming recollection: The later family notice repeats Gershom's name and Moses' statement that he was a sojourner in a foreign land, directly recalling the naming explanation here.
  Acts 7:29 — Retrospective family continuation: Stephen describes Moses fathering two sons during his Midian sojourn; the birth and foreign-land naming here introduce the first of that family without denying the later second son.

Exodus 2:23
After those many days the king of Egypt died. The sons of Israel groaned deeply because of their labour and cried out, and their cry because of their labour rose to God.
  Acts 7:34 — Explicit retrospective divine hearing: Stephen's bush speech recalls God hearing the people's groaning and seeing their Egyptian mistreatment, corresponding to the labour cry that rises to God here.
  Exodus 6:5 — Explicit divine recollection: God later states that He has heard the groaning of the people Egypt enslaves, recalling the cry produced by the labour condition narrated here.

Exodus 2:24
God heard their groaning, and God remembered His covenant with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.
  Genesis 15:13-18 — Covenant and announced affliction: The Abrahamic covenant scene announces descendants' foreign servitude, affliction and eventual return, supplying the earlier promise context for God's remembering the covenant amid Egyptian groaning.
  Exodus 6:3-5 — Explicit covenant recollection: The later divine speech again joins Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, the land covenant, Israel's groaning and remembrance, restating the particular covenant connection narrated here.
  Psalms 104:8-10 — Explicit retrospective covenant account: The psalm describes remembrance of the covenant and names Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, corresponding to the ancestral covenant God remembers in Exodus.

Exodus 2:25
God looked upon the sons of Israel, and He became known to them.
  Exodus 3:7 — Divine attention developed: The following call speech declares that God has seen the people's affliction and knows their pain, developing His attention to Israel while leaving the present became-known wording unchanged.
  Exodus 6:6-7 — Divine self-disclosure developed: God later promises deliverance and that Israel will know Him as their delivering God, corresponding to the supplied notice that He became known to them rather than silently reversing its direction of knowing.

Exodus 3:1
Moses was tending the sheep of Jethro his father-in-law, the priest of Midian. He led the sheep toward the wilderness and came to Mount Horeb.
  Acts 7:30 — Explicit retrospective call setting: Stephen places the burning-bush appearance in the wilderness of Mount Sinai, recalling this call setting while retaining the supplied Horeb and Sinai labels without an independent geographical reconstruction.
  Exodus 2:16 — Pastoral household background: The earlier account identifies Jethro as Midian's priest and names his daughters' flock work, supplying the household and livestock setting from which Moses now tends the sheep.

Exodus 3:2
An angel of the Lord appeared to him in a flame of fire from the bush. He saw that the bush was burning with fire, yet it was not consumed.
  Acts 7:30 — Explicit retrospective appearance: Stephen expressly recalls an angel appearing in the flame of a burning bush, matching the messenger and fire setting without replacing either passage's wording about the speaker.
  Deuteronomy 33:16 — Explicit bush allusion: Moses' later blessing invokes the One who appeared in the bush, recalling the distinctive appearance narrated here while the blessing itself concerns Joseph's family.

Exodus 3:3
And Moses said, “I will turn aside and see this great sight, why the bush is not consumed.”
  Exodus 3:2 — Same-chapter observed sign and response: Moses' stated wish to investigate follows the narrator's description of a burning bush that is not consumed, identifying the particular sight that draws him aside.
  Acts 7:31 — Explicit retrospective response: Stephen recalls Moses' amazement and approach to examine the sight, corresponding to his decision to look into the unconsumed bush.

Exodus 3:4
When the Lord saw that he was approaching to look, the Lord called to him from the bush, “Moses, Moses!” He replied, “What is it?”
  Acts 7:31-32 — Explicit retrospective divine call: Stephen describes the Lord's voice coming as Moses approaches and gives the ancestral-God declaration, recalling the call that interrupts his investigation.
  Genesis 46:2 — Repeated divine-call form: God's earlier double call Jacob, Jacob receives the same supplied What-is-it response, providing a particular call-and-answer parallel without identifying the two occasions.

Exodus 3:5
And He said, “Do not come near here. Untie the footwear from your feet; for the place where you stand is holy ground.”
  Acts 7:33 — Explicit retrospective quotation: Stephen repeats the command to remove footwear because the place is holy ground, recalling this instruction in the bush speech.
  Joshua 5:15 — Shared holy-ground instruction: The chief captain likewise commands Joshua to loosen his sandal because the place is holy, repeating the instruction pattern without independently identifying the two appearing figures.

Exodus 3:6
And He said to him, “I am the God of your father, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob.” And Moses turned his face away, for he feared to look before God.
  Matthew 22:31-32 — Explicit quotation and interpretation: Jesus quotes God's identification with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob in answering the resurrection question, giving an explicit later use of the ancestral-God declaration.
  Mark 12:26-27 — Explicit bush quotation and interpretation: Jesus expressly cites the book of Moses and the bush passage when naming Abraham's, Isaac's and Jacob's God, interpreting the declaration in relation to the living.
  Luke 20:37-38 — Explicit bush reference and interpretation: Jesus refers to Moses at the bush naming the Lord as the patriarchs' God, using this declaration in His account of resurrection and life to God.
  Acts 7:32 — Explicit retrospective declaration: Stephen repeats the ancestral-God words and Moses' reluctance to look, corresponding to both the self-identification and Moses' fearful response narrated here.

Exodus 3:7
And the Lord said to Moses, “I have surely seen the affliction of My people in Egypt, and have heard their cry because of their taskmasters; for I know their pain.
  Exodus 2:23-25 — Divine speech and prior narration: God's statement that He has seen affliction and heard the cry recalls the earlier narration of groaning, covenant remembrance and attention to Israel.
  Acts 7:34 — Explicit retrospective quotation: Stephen repeats God's seeing Egyptian mistreatment and hearing groaning, directly recalling the attention to suffering announced in this call speech.

Exodus 3:8
I have come down to rescue them from the hand of the Egyptians and bring them out of that land into a good and spacious land, a land flowing with milk and honey, to the place of the Canaanites, Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Girgashites, Hivites, and Jebusites.
  Genesis 15:18-21 — Ancestral land-covenant connection: The earlier covenant grants Abraham's descendants land associated with named peoples, supplying the land-promise background while its longer list is not forced into an identical enumeration.
  Deuteronomy 26:8-9 — Explicit retrospective deliverance and land: The later confession joins God's bringing Israel out of Egypt with His gift of a milk-and-honey land, recalling the paired rescue and destination announced here.
  Exodus 6:6-8 — Repeated deliverance-and-land promise: God later again promises rescue from Egyptian domination and entry into the land sworn to the patriarchs, corresponding to both parts of the announced divine action.

Exodus 3:9
And now, behold, the cry of the sons of Israel has come to Me; and I have seen the oppression with which the Egyptians oppress them.
  Exodus 2:23-25 — Divine speech and prior narration: The cry reaching God and His observation of oppression recall the earlier account of Israel's labour cry and God's attention to the people.
  Exodus 3:7 — Same-chapter explicit divine-speech repetition: God repeats the cry-and-oppression grounds already given for His intervention, returning to what He has heard and seen before commissioning Moses.

Exodus 3:10
Come now, and I will send you to Pharaoh king of Egypt; and you shall bring My people, the sons of Israel, out of the land of Egypt.”
  Acts 7:34 — Explicit retrospective commission: Stephen's bush speech repeats God's sending Moses to Egypt, recalling the particular commission to confront Pharaoh and bring Israel out.
  Psalms 104:26 — Explicit retrospective sending: The psalm names God's sending of Moses His servant, alongside Aaron, recalling the divinely initiated mission first commissioned here.

Exodus 3:11
Moses said to God, “Who am I, that I should go to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, and bring the sons of Israel out of the land of Egypt?”
  Exodus 3:12 — Same-chapter objection and divine answer: God answers Moses' question about his own adequacy with the promise of divine presence and a sign, identifying the response to this initial commission objection.
  Exodus 4:10 — Developed commission objection: Moses later cites weak voice and slow tongue as further grounds for hesitation, continuing his objections to the same mission without treating the two objections as identical words.

Exodus 3:12
And God said to Moses, “I will be with you; and this shall be the sign to you that I send you: when you have brought My people out of Egypt, you shall serve God upon this mountain.”
  Deuteronomy 4:10-12 — Promised mountain gathering and later recollection: The later Horeb recollection describes the assembled people hearing God at the mountain, corresponding to the announced post-deliverance encounter while preserving each passage's particular worship and hearing language.
  Joshua 1:5 — Explicit later presence comparison: God's promise to Joshua expressly appeals to His being with Moses, recalling the divine-presence assurance on which Moses' mission is grounded here.

Exodus 3:13
And Moses said to God, “Behold, I shall go to the sons of Israel and say to them, ‘The God of your fathers has sent me to you’; and they shall ask me, ‘What is His name?’ What shall I say to them?”
  Exodus 3:14-15 — Same-chapter name question and divine answer: The following One-Who-Is declaration and ancestral-Lord message answer Moses' question about what name he should give Israel, with both supplied formulations retained.
  Exodus 6:2-3 — Related divine-name disclosure: The later speech again gives the name Lord and discusses revelation to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, providing a related disclosure without independently reconciling every statement about prior knowledge.

Exodus 3:14
And God said to Moses, “I am the One Who Is.” And He said, “This is what you shall say to the sons of Israel: ‘The One Who Is has sent me to you.’”
  Exodus 3:13 — Same-chapter explicit answer to name question: God's instruction to say The One Who Is has sent Moses directly answers what he should tell the Israelites when they ask the sender's name.
  Exodus 3:15 — Same-chapter complementary divine messages: The following ancestral-Lord message continues what Moses must tell Israel and adds everlasting-name and memorial language, preserving it alongside the One-Who-Is formulation rather than substituting one for the other.

Exodus 3:15
And God said again to Moses, “This is what you shall say to the sons of Israel: ‘The Lord, the God of your fathers, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, has sent me to you.’ This is My everlasting name, and My memorial from generation to generation.
  Exodus 3:6 — Same-chapter repeated ancestral identification: The message Moses must give Israel repeats the God-of-Abraham-Isaac-Jacob identification already spoken to him, now specifying the sender in the commission.
  Psalms 134:13 — Shared everlasting-name and memorial formula: The psalm likewise declares the Lord's name forever and His memorial from generation to generation, closely corresponding to both enduring-name expressions in the commission message.

Exodus 3:16

  Exodus 4:29-31 — Commission and narrative implementation: Moses and Aaron later gather the elders and announce God's words, and the people believe that God has visited and seen affliction, implementing the assembly and message ordered here.
  Genesis 50:24-25 — Ancestral visitation promise developed: Joseph had promised God's visitation and departure to the patriarchs' sworn land; the present message announces that God has visited Israel's Egyptian condition, continuing that particular expectation.

Exodus 3:17
And I have said, I will bring you up out of the affliction of the Egyptians, into the land of the Canaanites, and Hittites, and Amorites, and Perizzites, and Girgashites, and Hivites, and Jebusites, into a land flowing with milk and honey.”’
  Exodus 3:8 — Same-chapter repeated rescue destination: The message for the elders repeats the earlier promise to bring Israel from Egypt's affliction to the named peoples' land flowing with milk and honey.
  Deuteronomy 26:8-9 — Explicit retrospective deliverance and destination: The later confession recalls God's bringing Israel out of Egypt and giving a milk-and-honey land, corresponding to the rescue and land announced to the elders.

Exodus 3:18

  Exodus 5:1-3 — Instruction and narrated petition: Moses and Aaron later request wilderness worship and a three-day sacrifice journey before Pharaoh, implementing the petition while not silently adding elders to that later scene's named participants.
  Exodus 4:29-31 — Predicted hearing and narrated response: The elders are assembled and the people believe the visitation message, corresponding to the promised reception while retaining Aaron's actual speaking role.

Exodus 3:19
I know that Pharaoh, king of Egypt, will not let you go unless by a strong hand.
  Exodus 5:1-2 — Predicted refusal and narrative response: Pharaoh later refuses the request and declares he will not let Israel go, providing the narrated resistance God predicts here.
  Exodus 6:1 — Repeated strong-hand deliverance assurance: God later says a mighty hand will cause Pharaoh to release and drive Israel out, developing the strong-hand qualification attached to Pharaoh's resistance here.

Exodus 3:20
I will stretch out My hand and strike the Egyptians with all My wonders that I will perform among them. Afterward he will send you away.

Exodus 3:21

  Exodus 12:35-36 — Promise and narrative fulfilment: Israel's requests for silver, gold and clothing and the Egyptians' favourable lending supply the narrated outcome of not departing empty-handed.

Exodus 3:22
Every woman shall ask of her neighbour and of her fellow lodger articles of silver and gold, and clothing; and you shall put them upon your sons and upon your daughters, and you shall plunder the Egyptians.”
  Exodus 12:35-36 — Instruction and narrative fulfilment: The later account expressly records the requests for silver, gold and clothing and describes the Egyptians as plundered, carrying out the material departure instruction given here.
  Genesis 15:14 — Ancestral departure promise developed: The earlier promise says the afflicted descendants will leave their serving nation with much baggage; the requested Egyptian goods provide the later specified possessions without replacing that supplied wording with another edition's phrase.

Exodus 4:1
And Moses answered and said, “If they do not believe me or listen to my voice, for they will say, ‘God has not appeared to you,’ what shall I say to them?”
  Exodus 3:18 — Objection and prior assurance: Moses' fear that Israel will not believe or listen stands beside God's preceding promise that the elders will listen, preserving the difference between the human objection and divine assurance.
  Exodus 4:31 — Same-chapter feared unbelief and narrated belief: The people eventually believe and worship after the words and signs, supplying the narrated contrast to Moses' initial fear that his claim of an appearance will be rejected.

Exodus 4:2
And the Lord said to him, “What is this in your hand?” And he said, “A staff.”
  Exodus 4:17 — Same-chapter staff identification and commission: The later instruction identifies this staff as the one turned into a serpent and directs Moses to take it for signs, developing the object introduced in the question here.
  Exodus 4:20 — Same-chapter staff carried on departure: Moses later takes the staff he had from God as he returns toward Egypt, carrying the object introduced before the verification signs into the mission journey.

Exodus 4:3
He said, “Throw it on the ground.” He threw it on the ground, and it became a serpent; Moses fled from it.

  Exodus 7:9-12 — Distinct staff-transformation scenes: Aaron later throws his staff before Pharaoh and it becomes a dragon, providing a related public sign while preserving the different performer, setting and supplied serpent-versus-dragon wording.

Exodus 4:4

  Exodus 4:3 — Same-chapter reversed transformation: The return to a staff reverses the preceding transformation into a serpent, with Moses' obedience replacing his earlier flight from it.
  Exodus 4:17 — Same-chapter transformed staff later identified: The later command names the staff turned into a serpent as the instrument for signs, recalling the transformation and restoration completed here.

Exodus 4:5
“That they may believe you, that the Lord, the God of their fathers, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, has appeared to you.”

Exodus 4:6
And the Lord said again to him, “Put your hand into your bosom.” And he put his hand into his bosom, and brought his hand out of his bosom; and his hand became as snow.
  Exodus 4:7 — Same-chapter altered hand and restoration: The next instruction restores the hand to its flesh colour, reversing the as-snow appearance without supplying a medical diagnosis absent from this verse.
  Numbers 12:10 — Shared snow comparison with distinct descriptions: Miriam's condition is expressly called leprous as snow, providing the particular snow comparison while Moses' hand here is described simply as becoming as snow; no independent modern diagnosis is imposed.

Exodus 4:7
And He said, “Put your hand again into your bosom.” And he put his hand into his bosom, and brought it out of his bosom; and it was restored again to the colour of his flesh.
  Exodus 4:6 — Same-chapter restored hand and preceding change: The restored flesh colour reverses the hand's previous as-snow state, completing the second verification scene through the repeated bosom action.
  Exodus 4:8 — Same-chapter hand sign and belief purpose: God next speaks of belief through the latter sign, placing the hand's alteration and restoration within the proposed verification of Moses' message.

Exodus 4:8
“And if they do not believe you or listen to the voice of the first sign, they shall believe you through the voice of the latter sign.
  Exodus 4:1 — Same-chapter signs answer feared unbelief: The conditional instruction about believing signs responds to Moses' earlier concern that the people will neither believe him nor listen to his voice.
  Exodus 4:9 — Same-chapter conditional verification expanded: The following instruction adds river water becoming blood if the first two signs do not secure belief, retaining the successive conditions rather than claiming unconditional performance of every sign here.

Exodus 4:9

  Exodus 7:17-21 — Distinct water-to-blood sign scenes: The later plague turns Egyptian waters to blood through the commanded staff action; the shared transformation is compared while the small poured-water sign and larger river judgment remain distinct.
  Exodus 4:30 — Same-chapter commanded signs and public presentation: The later assembly records Aaron speaking and performing signs before the people, supplying a public presentation of the commanded verification without individually enumerating which signs he performs there.

Exodus 4:10
And Moses said to the Lord, “I beseech Thee, Lord, I have not been capable—not yesterday, nor the day before, nor since Thou didst begin to speak to Thy servant; for I am weak of voice and slow of tongue.”
  Exodus 6:12 — Repeated speech objection: Moses later again doubts Pharaoh will listen because he is without eloquence, recalling his protest about weak voice and slow tongue while preserving the different wording.
  Exodus 4:12 — Same-chapter speech difficulty and divine answer: God answers the voice-and-tongue objection by promising to open Moses' mouth and teach his speech, identifying the commission's stated provision rather than a new medical explanation.
  Acts 7:22 — Distinct perspectives on Moses' speech: Stephen describes Moses as educated in Egyptian wisdom and powerful in words and deeds, whereas Moses here objects that his voice and tongue are inadequate; the different assessments remain without an invented resolution.

Exodus 4:11
The Lord said to Moses, “Who gave a human being a mouth? Who made one hard of hearing or deaf, seeing or blind? Is it not I, God?
  Psalms 93:9 — Shared sensory-creation argument: The psalm appeals to God as planter of the ear and former of the eye, corresponding to the divine appeal to the maker of human mouth, hearing and sight in answering Moses.
  Exodus 4:12 — Same-chapter creator claim and promised assistance: The assertion about God's making human faculties is followed by the promise to open Moses' mouth and teach him, giving the specific commission response grounded in that assertion.

Exodus 4:12
Now therefore go, and I will open your mouth, and teach you what you are to speak.”
  Exodus 4:10 — Same-chapter answer to speech objection: The promise to open and teach Moses' mouth responds to his stated weak voice and slow tongue without replacing his supplied description with a later diagnosis.
  Jeremiah 1:6-9 — Prophetic commission and speech assistance parallel: Jeremiah likewise protests inability to speak and receives divine commissioning, presence and words in his mouth, providing a related speech-assistance pattern while his youth differs from Moses' objection.

Exodus 4:13
And Moses said, “I beseech Thee, Lord, appoint another who is able, whom Thou wilt send.”

  Exodus 4:14 — Same-chapter request and divine response: The account answers Moses' request with God's anger and the provision of Aaron as a speaking brother, preserving the particular response rather than making Moses' replacement request succeed as stated.

Exodus 4:14

Exodus 4:16

Exodus 4:17

Exodus 4:18

Exodus 4:19

  Exodus 2:15 — Earlier threat and return permission: Pharaoh earlier sought to kill Moses, occasioning his withdrawal to Midian; the new instruction permits return because those seeking his life are dead.
  Exodus 2:23 — Repeated royal-death notice: The earlier notice likewise states that Egypt's king died after many days; the matching circumstance accompanies permission to return here without an independently reconstructed sequence of royal reigns.
  Matthew 2:19-21 — Parallel return instruction with different destinations: The angel later tells Joseph to take the Child back to Israel because His life-seekers have died, echoing the threat-ended return pattern while Moses returns to Egypt and the family leaves it.

Exodus 4:20
Moses took his wife and children, set them on the beasts of burden, and returned toward Egypt. Moses took the staff he had from God in his hand.

Exodus 4:21

  Exodus 7:3-5 — Repeated hardening and sign commission: The later divine speech again joins Pharaoh's hardened resistance, multiplied signs and God's intervention to bring Israel out, restating the main elements announced here.

Exodus 4:22

  Hosea 11:1 — Related Israel-child and Egypt language: Hosea's divine speech recalls God loving Israel as an infant and summoning his children from Egypt, corresponding to the collective family-and-Egypt setting while the supplied plural children is not rewritten as a singular quotation.

Exodus 4:23
And I said to you, “Let My people go, that they may serve Me.” If therefore you will not let them go, behold, I will kill your firstborn son.’”

Exodus 4:24

Exodus 4:25
And Zipporah took a stone, and circumcised the foreskin of her son, and fell at the feet, and said, “The blood of my child’s circumcision has ceased.”

Exodus 4:26
And he departed from him, because she said, “The blood of my child’s circumcision has ceased.”
  Exodus 4:24-25 — Same-chapter explicit encounter and speech recollection: The departure notice repeats Zipporah's blood-of-my-child's-circumcision-has-ceased words and follows the threatening encounter and action, preserving both the repetition and unresolved pronouns without substituting a bridegroom formula.

Exodus 4:27
The Lord said to Aaron, “Go into the wilderness to meet Moses.” He went and met him at the mountain of God, and they kissed each other.

Exodus 4:28

Exodus 4:29

  Exodus 4:30-31 — Same-chapter assembly and narrated reception: The gathered council is followed by Aaron's words and signs and the people's belief and worship, supplying the public purpose and response of the assembly.

Exodus 4:30
Aaron spoke all these words God had spoken to Moses and performed the signs before the people.

Exodus 4:31
And the people believed and rejoiced, because God had visited the sons of Israel, and because He had seen their affliction; and the people bowed down and worshipped.
