Exodus 5:1
Afterward Moses and Aaron went in to Pharaoh and said, “Thus says the Lord, the God of Israel: ‘Let My people go so that they may hold a feast for Me in the wilderness.’”
  Exodus 3:18 — Commission and actual royal petition: The wilderness-sacrifice petition follows the earlier instruction to approach Pharaoh, while the actual audience names Moses and Aaron rather than silently adding the council of elders to the scene.
  Exodus 12:31-32 — Request and eventual permission: Pharaoh eventually orders Moses, Aaron and Israel to depart and serve the Lord, reversing his refusal of the worship-related release first requested here.

Exodus 5:2
Pharaoh said, “Who is He, that I should obey His voice and let the sons of Israel go? I do not know the Lord, and I will not let Israel go.”
  Exodus 3:19 — Predicted and actual refusal: God had announced that Pharaoh would not release Israel without a strong hand; Pharaoh now explicitly refuses, giving an initial narrated instance of that resistance.
  Exodus 7:5 — Claimed ignorance and announced knowledge: Pharaoh says he does not know the Lord; God later announces that the Egyptians will know Him through His intervention, setting the royal claim beside the stated purpose of the signs without equating every Egyptian with Pharaoh.

Exodus 5:3
They said to him, “The God of the Hebrews has called us. We will go a three days’ journey into the wilderness to sacrifice to our God, lest death or slaughter meet us.”
  Exodus 3:18 — Earlier instruction repeated: The God-of-the-Hebrews message and three-day wilderness sacrifice request closely follow the instruction Moses received, while the present death-or-slaughter warning remains an additional part of the petition.
  Exodus 8:23 — Repeated three-day worship request: Moses later again insists on a three-day wilderness journey to sacrifice as instructed, repeating the particular destination and purpose of the present petition.

Exodus 5:4
And the king of Egypt said to them, “Moses and Aaron, why do you turn my people from their works? Each of you, go back to your work.”
  Exodus 5:6-9 — Same-chapter royal objection and labour policy: Pharaoh's complaint that Moses and Aaron turn people from work is followed by orders withdrawing straw and increasing pressure, developing his stated opposition into a specific labour policy.

Exodus 5:5
And Pharaoh said, “Behold, the people are now numerous; let us not therefore give them rest from their works.”
  Exodus 1:9-10 — Royal population arguments compared: The opening royal speech uses Israel's numbers as grounds for restrictive policy; Pharaoh here invokes the people's numbers against allowing rest. The two speeches share that argument without independently establishing the identity or reign of their speakers.
  Exodus 5:8 — Same-chapter refusal of rest and idleness accusation: The refusal to give rest is followed by Pharaoh's accusation that the workers are idle and therefore ask to sacrifice, developing his own explanation rather than a narrator's assessment of their diligence.

Exodus 5:6
Pharaoh commanded the people’s taskmasters and scribes:
  Exodus 5:7-9 — Same-chapter command introduction and content: The following words specify what Pharaoh commands the taskmasters and scribes: no supplied straw, the same brick count and heavier tasks, completing the command introduced here.
  Exodus 5:10-11 — Same-chapter command and its transmission: The taskmasters and scribes later convey Pharaoh's straw and quota terms to the people, showing the named officials delivering the command they receive here.

Exodus 5:7
“The people shall no longer be given straw for making bricks as before. Let them go and gather straw for themselves.
  Exodus 5:10-12 — Same-chapter straw order and implementation: The officers repeat the withdrawal of supplied straw and the people scatter to gather stubble, carrying the new material-gathering requirement into the narrated work without identifying stubble and provided straw as identical descriptions.
  Exodus 5:18 — Same-chapter policy reaffirmed: After the scribes complain, Pharaoh again refuses supplied straw while requiring the appointed bricks, reaffirming rather than withdrawing the policy announced here.

Exodus 5:8
And you shall lay upon them the appointed number of bricks which they make each day; you shall not reduce it at all. For they are idle; therefore they cry out, saying, ‘Let us go and sacrifice to our God.’
  Exodus 5:13-14 — Same-chapter quota and enforced consequence: The unchanged daily brick requirement is enforced by the taskmasters and followed by the scribes' scourging for an unmet count, connecting the royal requirement with its narrated pressure and punishment.
  Exodus 5:17 — Same-chapter royal accusation repeated: Pharaoh later repeats the idleness charge and links it to the request to sacrifice, preserving the allegation as his recurring explanation rather than the narrator's verdict.

Exodus 5:9
Let these men’s tasks be heavier. Let them attend to them and pay no attention to empty words.”
  Exodus 1:14 — Existing oppression intensified: The earlier narration describes painful brick, clay and field labour; Pharaoh now orders heavier tasks, intensifying the particular work regime already imposed on Israel.
  Exodus 5:14 — Same-chapter heavier tasks and punishment: The subsequent scourging of the Israelite scribes for unmet bricks gives a specific narrated consequence within the heavier-task regime Pharaoh orders here.

Exodus 5:10
The taskmasters and scribes pressed them and said to the people, “Thus says Pharaoh: ‘I will no longer give you straw.
  Exodus 5:6-9 — Same-chapter royal order reported to workers: The officials' Thus-says-Pharaoh announcement delivers the straw withdrawal contained in the preceding royal instructions, linking their message to its stated source.

Exodus 5:11
Go yourselves, and gather straw for yourselves wherever you may find it; for your appointed number shall not be reduced.’”
  Exodus 5:7-8 — Same-chapter policy terms transmitted: The officials repeat both the requirement to gather straw and the unchanged brick count, carrying the two parts of Pharaoh's policy into their message to the people.
  Exodus 5:12 — Same-chapter gathering order and response: The people's scattering to gather stubble follows the command to obtain material themselves, recording the practical response to the new requirement.

Exodus 5:12
The people scattered throughout the land of Egypt to gather stubble for straw.
  Exodus 5:7 — Same-chapter ordered and actual gathering: Pharaoh ordered the workers to gather their own straw; the narrator now reports their search for stubble throughout Egypt, distinguishing the issued requirement from the material-gathering action described.
  Exodus 5:16 — Same-chapter gathering and later supply complaint: The workers gather material themselves, and the scribes later complain that straw is not supplied. The two statements distinguish compulsory gathering from the former provision rather than claiming no material of any kind exists.

Exodus 5:13
The taskmasters pressed them, saying, “Complete the work due each day, as when straw was provided for you.”
  Exodus 5:8 — Same-chapter daily requirement enforced: The taskmasters' demand for each day's work repeats Pharaoh's instruction not to reduce the daily brick count, identifying the authority behind their pressure.
  Exodus 5:14 — Same-chapter pressure and scourging: The demand for daily completion is followed by punishment and a question about the unfinished count, showing how the stated pressure is enforced upon the Israelite scribes.

Exodus 5:14
And the scribes of the people of the sons of Israel, whom Pharaoh’s overseers had set over them, were scourged, and asked, “Why have you not completed your appointed number of bricks, as before, including that of today?”
  Exodus 5:15-16 — Same-chapter beating and subsequent complaint: The scribes' petition to Pharaoh reports the same scourging and unprovided straw whose circumstances the narrator has described, keeping their complaint tied to the preceding events.
  Exodus 5:8 — Same-chapter punishment and unchanged quota: The accusation of failing to complete bricks implements Pharaoh's unchanged-count requirement, making the earlier policy the context of the narrated beating.

Exodus 5:15
And the scribes of the sons of Israel came in and cried to Pharaoh, saying, “Why do you treat your household slaves this way?
  Exodus 5:14 — Same-chapter punished officials seek an audience: The Israelite scribes who have been scourged now come to Pharaoh and ask why they are treated this way, connecting the petitioners with the preceding punishment.
  Exodus 5:17-18 — Same-chapter petition and royal answer: Pharaoh answers the scribes' complaint with the idleness accusation and the same straw-and-brick policy, supplying the actual response to their appeal.

Exodus 5:16
Straw is not given to your household slaves, yet they tell us to make bricks; and behold, your servants have been scourged. In this way you will wrong your own people.”
  Exodus 5:7-8 — Same-chapter complaint and documented royal policy: The complaint that straw is not supplied while bricks are demanded corresponds to the two requirements Pharaoh himself ordered, rather than an invented account of the policy.
  Exodus 5:14 — Same-chapter complaint recalls scourging: The scribes' statement that the servants have been scourged recalls the narrator's earlier report of their beating, preserving the distinction between the occurrence and their petition about it.

Exodus 5:17
And he said to them, “You are idle; you are idlers! Therefore you say, ‘Let us go and sacrifice to our God.’
  Exodus 5:8 — Same-chapter idleness allegation repeated: The repeated You-are-idle accusation returns to Pharaoh's earlier claim that the sacrifice request arises from idleness, not an independent narrative confirmation of that charge.

Exodus 5:18
Now therefore go and work; for straw shall not be given to you, yet you shall deliver the appointed number of bricks.”
  Exodus 5:7-8 — Same-chapter original policy retained: Pharaoh repeats the refusal of straw and the appointed brick count after the complaint, retaining both original terms despite the workers' request for relief.
  Exodus 5:19 — Same-chapter answer and acknowledged trouble: The scribes recognize their trouble after Pharaoh reaffirms the unchanged daily requirement, connecting his answer with their stated assessment of its consequences.

Exodus 5:19
And the scribes of the sons of Israel saw themselves in trouble, saying, “You shall not reduce the number of bricks due each day.”
  Exodus 5:18 — Same-chapter repeated quota and response: The scribes' recognition that the daily bricks cannot be reduced follows Pharaoh's renewed requirement, making their distress a response to the actual answer they receive.

Exodus 5:20
As they came out from Pharaoh, they met Moses and Aaron, who were coming to meet them.
  Exodus 5:21 — Same-chapter meeting and complaint: The encounter with Moses and Aaron immediately leads into the scribes' accusation that the brothers have endangered them, supplying the content and purpose of this reported meeting.

Exodus 5:21
And they said to them, “May God look upon you and judge, for you have made our smell loathsome before Pharaoh and before his attendants, putting a sword into his hands to kill us.”
  Exodus 5:22-23 — Same-chapter accusation and ensuing appeal to God: The scribes blame Moses and Aaron for worsening their danger; Moses then asks the Lord about the people's increased affliction and lack of deliverance, carrying the crisis into a different speaker's appeal without treating the accusations as identical words.

Exodus 5:22
And Moses returned to the Lord and said, “Lord, why hast Thou afflicted this people? And why hast Thou sent me?
  Exodus 3:10 — Commission recalled in Moses' question: God had sent Moses to Pharaoh to bring Israel out; Moses now asks why he was sent after the oppression worsens, referring his complaint back to that particular commission.
  Exodus 6:1 — Appeal and divine reply: The Lord's next answer promises action against Pharaoh and release by a mighty hand, responding to Moses' question about the affliction and purpose of his sending.

Exodus 5:23
For since I went to Pharaoh to speak in Thy name, he has afflicted this people; and Thou hast not delivered Thy people.”
  Exodus 5:6-9 — Same-chapter complaint and preceding policy: Moses says the people have suffered since the royal approach; the chapter has already narrated Pharaoh's heavier labour and straw restrictions after that audience, supplying the particular worsening behind the complaint.
  Exodus 6:1 — Present complaint and renewed deliverance assurance: Moses says deliverance has not occurred; God's immediate reply announces what He will now do to Pharaoh, keeping the present complaint distinct from the renewed promise and later outcome.

Exodus 6:1
And the Lord said to Moses, “Now you shall see what I will do to Pharaoh; for with a mighty hand he shall let them go, and with an uplifted arm he shall drive them out of his land.”
  Exodus 5:22-23 — Divine reply to the deliverance complaint: God's mighty-hand release assurance answers Moses' preceding questions about increased affliction and the absence of deliverance, rather than describing the people as already released.
  Exodus 12:31-33 — Assured and eventual forced departure: Pharaoh later orders departure and the Egyptians press the people to leave quickly, supplying the eventual release-and-driving-out outcome announced here.

Exodus 6:2
God spoke to Moses and said to him, “I am the Lord.
  Exodus 3:14-15 — Earlier divine self-identification: The bush commission gives both the One-Who-Is message and the ancestral-Lord message; the renewed speech opens with I-am-the-Lord, continuing the divine identification without replacing the earlier supplied formulations.

Exodus 6:3
I appeared to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob as their God, but I did not reveal My name Lord to them.
  Genesis 17:1 — Earlier patriarchal appearance and title: The earlier appearance to Abram includes the supplied words I-am-your-God; the present speech recalls appearing to the patriarchs as their God, without importing an Almighty title absent from these English records.
  Exodus 3:13-15 — Divine-name statements compared: The bush speech instructs Moses to announce the name Lord and the ancestral identification; this renewed speech says that name was not revealed to the patriarchs. Both supplied statements remain without an independent reconciliation of every earlier naming passage.

Exodus 6:4
I established My covenant with them, to give them the land of the Canaanites, the land where they sojourned and lived as foreigners.
  Genesis 17:7-8 — Earlier covenant and sojourning land promise: God's covenant with Abraham and his seed promises Canaan, where he lives as a stranger; the present recollection likewise joins covenant, land and the ancestors' foreign sojourn.
  Genesis 35:12 — Land promise carried through the patriarchs: God earlier names the land given to Abraham and Isaac and promises it to Jacob and his seed, supplying a particular antecedent to the ancestral land covenant recalled here.

Exodus 6:5
I have heard the groaning of the sons of Israel, whom the Egyptians enslave, and I have remembered your covenant.
  Exodus 2:23-25 — Earlier hearing and covenant remembrance: The earlier narrator joins Israel's groaning, divine hearing and remembrance of the covenant with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob; God now recounts hearing and remembering, with the supplied your-covenant wording retained.
  Psalms 104:8-10 — Later recollection of the ancestral covenant: The psalm recounts covenant remembrance and explicitly names Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, corresponding to the ancestral commitment recalled alongside the Egyptian groaning here.

Exodus 6:6
Go, say to the sons of Israel: ‘I am the Lord. I will bring you out from the Egyptians’ domination and deliver you from slavery. I will ransom you with an uplifted arm and with great judgment.
  Exodus 3:7-8 — Repeated rescue announcement: The bush speech promises rescue from Egyptian domination; the renewed message again announces removal from that domination and slavery, adding its supplied ransom-and-uplifted-arm formulation.
  Deuteronomy 26:8 — Later recollection of powerful deliverance: The later confession describes the Lord bringing Israel out with mighty hand, uplifted arm, signs and wonders, recalling the powerful deliverance promised here.

Exodus 6:7
And I will take you to Myself for a people, and I will be your God; and you shall know that I am the Lord your God, who brought you out from the oppression of the Egyptians.
  Leviticus 26:12-13 — Repeated people-and-God formula: Leviticus likewise promises that Israel will be God's people and He their God, then recalls bringing them out while they were slaves, paralleling the relationship and Egyptian deliverance joined here.
  Exodus 29:45-46 — Later knowledge and delivering-God declaration: The later speech again says Israel will know the Lord as the God who brought them out of Egypt and will be their God, closely repeating the relationship and recognition promised here without changing its called-upon wording to dwelling language.

Exodus 6:8
I will bring you into the land concerning which I stretched out My hand to give it to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. I will give it to you as an inheritance. I am the Lord.’”
  Genesis 35:12 — Specified ancestral land promise: The earlier promise explicitly joins Abraham, Isaac, Jacob and the land to be given to descendants, corresponding to the three patriarchs and inheritance now named in the deliverance message.
  Deuteronomy 26:9 — Promised inheritance and later confession: The later confession states that God brought Israel into the land and gave it to them, providing a later account of possession to compare with the inheritance promised here.

Exodus 6:9
Moses spoke thus to the sons of Israel, but they did not listen to him because of their discouragement and hard labour.
  Exodus 4:31 — Changed reception of the message: The people previously believe, rejoice and worship upon hearing of God's visitation; here they do not listen because of discouragement and hard labour, preserving the narrator's particular explanation for the changed response.
  Exodus 5:18-19 — Work conditions preceding discouragement: Pharaoh has reaffirmed the brick count without straw and the scribes recognize their trouble, giving the immediate labour context before the narrator explains Israel's failure to listen through discouragement and work.

Exodus 6:10
The Lord spoke to Moses, saying,
  Exodus 6:11 — Same-chapter divine speech and its content: The following verse supplies the instruction to speak to Pharaoh for Israel's release, completing the divine-speech introduction rather than an unrelated command.

Exodus 6:11
“Go in and speak to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, so that he will let the sons of Israel leave his land.”
  Exodus 3:10 — Commission renewed: God's earlier sending to Pharaoh is renewed in the instruction to speak so that Israel may leave Egypt, continuing the same deliverance mission after the failed first audience.
  Exodus 7:2 — Renewed mission and speaking roles: The later instruction specifies Moses transmitting the commanded words and Aaron speaking to Pharaoh so Israel may go, developing the royal-speaking assignment given here.

Exodus 6:12
And Moses spoke before the Lord, saying, “Behold, the sons of Israel have not listened to me; how then shall Pharaoh listen to me? And I am without eloquence.”
  Exodus 4:10 — Repeated personal speech objection: Moses earlier calls himself weak of voice and slow of tongue; he now describes himself as without eloquence, preserving the different supplied expressions rather than replacing either with another edition's body metaphor or a diagnosis.
  Exodus 6:9 — Same-chapter objection cites the preceding response: Moses' statement that Israel has not listened recalls the narrator's immediately preceding account of that refusal, while the inference about Pharaoh remains Moses' own argument.

Exodus 6:13
The Lord spoke to Moses and Aaron and charged them concerning Pharaoh, king of Egypt, so that the sons of Israel would be sent out of the land of Egypt.
  Exodus 6:26-27 — Same-chapter commission frames the genealogy: The later summary identifies Aaron and Moses as those God commissioned and who addressed Pharaoh, returning to the two commissioned men after the intervening family entries.
  Exodus 7:1-2 — Commission and developed intermediary roles: The subsequent divine speech assigns Moses and Aaron their particular roles in speaking before Pharaoh, elaborating the shared deliverance charge stated here.

Exodus 6:14
These are the heads of their ancestral houses. The sons of Reuben, Israel’s firstborn: Hanoch, Pallu, Hezron, and Carmi. This is the kindred of Reuben.
  Genesis 46:9 — Parallel Reuben-family list: Both genealogies name Hanoch, Pallu, Hezron and Carmi as Reuben's sons, repeating the same four names in the migration and ancestral-house records.
  Numbers 26:5-6 — Family names and census clans: The later census names the Hanoch, Pallu, Hezron and Carmi clans under Reuben, carrying the four family branches into the census record.

Exodus 6:15
The sons of Simeon: Jemuel, Jamin, Ohad, Jachin, Zohar, and Shaul, the son of a Phoenician woman. These are the families of the sons of Simeon.
  Genesis 46:10 — Parallel Simeon list with retained differences: Both lists share Jemuel, Jamin, Jachin and Shaul, but Genesis supplies Aod and Saar and a Canaanite mother, whereas Exodus supplies Ohad and Zohar and a Phoenician mother. The different names and descriptions are not harmonized away.
  Numbers 26:12-13 — Simeon family and census compared: The census gives five Simeon clan headings, including Nemuel and Zerah, while Exodus gives six sons, including Jemuel, Ohad and Zohar. The shared family branch is compared with differing forms and membership preserved.

Exodus 6:16
These are the names of the sons of Levi according to their kindreds: Gershon, Kohath, and Merari. Levi lived one hundred and thirty-seven years.
  Genesis 46:11 — Parallel Levi-family list: The migration catalogue likewise names Gershon, Kohath and Merari as Levi's sons, identifying the three branches developed in this ancestral-house genealogy.
  Numbers 3:17 — Repeated Levite branches: Numbers again introduces Levi's Gershon, Kohath and Merari branches before describing their divisions and work, corresponding to the three sons listed here.

Exodus 6:17
These are the sons of Gershon: Libni and Shimei, the houses of their family.
  Numbers 3:18 — Repeated Gershon-family entry: Numbers names Libni and Shimei as Gershon's sons, directly corresponding to the two ancestral houses given here.
  1 Chronicles 6:2 — Parallel family record with different forms: Chronicles gives Gedson's Lobeni and Semei, while Exodus supplies Gershon's Libni and Shimei. The corresponding Levite family entries retain their actual name forms without a new original-language identity claim.

Exodus 6:18
The sons of Kohath: Amram, Izhar, Hebron, and Uzziel. Kohath lived one hundred and thirty years.
  Numbers 3:19 — Repeated Kohath-family branches: Numbers likewise names Amram, Izhar, Hebron and Uzziel under Kohath, repeating the four branches given here while not supplying this verse's lifespan notice.
  1 Chronicles 6:3 — Parallel Kohath-family record: Chronicles lists Kaath's Ambram, Issaar, Hebron and Oziel; Exodus retains Kohath, Amram, Izhar and Uzziel as supplied, allowing comparison of the family records without silently replacing the names.

Exodus 6:19
The sons of Merari: Mahli and Mushi. These are the ancestral houses of Levi according to their kindreds.
  Numbers 3:20 — Repeated Merari-family entry: Numbers again gives Mahli and Mushi as Merari's sons and identifies the Levite ancestral-house divisions, corresponding to the same two branches listed here.
  1 Chronicles 6:4 — Parallel Merari-family record: Chronicles supplies Merari's Mooli and Omousi before its ancestral-family summary, while Exodus supplies Mahli and Mushi. The shared branch and differently supplied forms remain distinct.

Exodus 6:20
Amram took Jochebed, the daughter of his father’s brother, as his wife, and she bore him Aaron, Moses, and their sister Miriam. Amram lived one hundred and thirty-two years.
  Exodus 2:1 — Named and previously unnamed Levite parents: The birth narrative introduces a Levite man and a daughter of Levi without personal names; this genealogy identifies Amram and Jochebed and names Aaron, Moses and Miriam, adding family detail without rewriting the earlier introduction.
  Numbers 26:59 — Parallel named-parent record with ancestry limits: Numbers likewise names Jochebed, Amram and their Aaron, Moses and Miriam, while its daughter-of-Levi and birth-in-Egypt clauses remain alongside Exodus's father's-brother's-daughter wording without an independently reconstructed ancestry.

Exodus 6:21
The sons of Izhar: Korah, Nepheg, and Zichri.
  Numbers 16:1 — Later identification of Korah's ancestry: The rebellion account explicitly calls Korah son of Izhar, son of Kohath, son of Levi, recalling the branch whose Izhar-and-Korah relationship is supplied here.
  Exodus 6:18 — Same-chapter branch extended by one generation: The earlier entry names Izhar among Kohath's sons; the present entry supplies Izhar's sons, linking this detailed branch to its preceding paternal heading.

Exodus 6:22
The sons of Uzziel: Elzaphan and Sithri.
  Numbers 3:30 — Later leadership notice in the Uzziel branch: Numbers names Elizaphan son of Uzziel as leader of the Kohath divisions, providing a later father-and-son leadership notice to compare with Exodus's supplied Elzaphan name.
  Leviticus 10:4 — Related Uzziel-family record with different membership: Leviticus names Misadai and Elzaphan as Uzziel's sons in the burial commission, while Exodus lists Elzaphan and Sithri. The shared Elzaphan-and-Uzziel relationship is retained without identifying the differently named additional sons as one person.

Exodus 6:23
Aaron took Elisheba, daughter of Amminadab and sister of Nahshon, as his wife. She bore him Nadab, Abihu, Eleazar, and Ithamar.
  Numbers 3:2 — Repeated Aaron-family list: Numbers likewise names Nadab, Abihu, Eleazar and Ithamar as Aaron's sons, corresponding to the four children attributed here to his marriage to Elisheba.
  Numbers 1:7 — Elisheba's paternal family identified elsewhere: The census names Nahshon son of Amminadab as Judah's representative, corresponding to the father and brother through whom this verse identifies Elisheba without transferring Nahshon's office to her.

Exodus 6:24
The sons of Korah: Assir, Elkanah, and Abiasaph. These are the generations of Korah.
  Numbers 26:11 — Later survival notice for Korah's sons: The census explicitly says Korah's sons did not die, supplying a later statement about the family group whose names are introduced here without claiming that the census repeats every child's name.
  1 Chronicles 6:7-8 — Related Korah-family record with a different structure: Chronicles presents Kore, Asir, Elkanah and Abiasaph within a his-son descent chain; Exodus lists Assir, Elkanah and Abiasaph as Korah's sons. The related records are compared without flattening the different generations or membership.

Exodus 6:25
Eleazar son of Aaron took one of Putiel’s daughters as his wife, and she bore him Phinehas. These are the heads of the Levites’ family according to their generations.
  Numbers 25:7-13 — Named child and later priestly episode: Numbers explicitly identifies Phinehas as Eleazar's son and Aaron's grandson and later reports a priesthood covenant for him, continuing the family history introduced by his birth here without adding a name for Putiel's daughter.
  Numbers 3:32 — Later leadership of Eleazar: Numbers names Eleazar son of Aaron as ruler over the Levite rulers and keeper of the holy charge, providing a particular later office for the father named in this family-head summary.

Exodus 6:26
These are Aaron and Moses whom God told to bring the sons of Israel out of the land of Egypt with their forces.
  Exodus 6:13 — Same-chapter commission recalled after genealogy: The identification of Aaron and Moses returns to God's earlier charge to those two men, linking the family account's closing identification with its pre-genealogy commission.
  Numbers 3:1 — Later Aaron-and-Moses genealogy heading: Numbers likewise introduces the generations of Aaron and Moses with a notice of God's speaking, recalling the same two commissioned family heads while retaining its later Mount Sinai setting.

Exodus 6:27
They were the ones who spoke to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, and brought the sons of Israel out of the land of Egypt: Aaron himself and Moses.
  Exodus 5:1 — Genealogical summary and earlier royal audience: The summary identifies Aaron and Moses as the men who spoke to Pharaoh; the preceding audience already narrates those two presenting God's release request, without making that first petition itself the actual departure.
  Exodus 12:31-37 — Summary identification and eventual departure: Pharaoh eventually addresses Moses and Aaron with permission to depart, and Israel leaves Rameses. That later sequence supplies the actual departure associated with the commissioned men in this genealogical summary.

Exodus 6:28
On the day when the Lord spoke to Moses in the land of Egypt,
  Exodus 6:29 — Same-chapter introductory setting completed: The following verse supplies the Lord's words to Moses in the Egyptian setting introduced here, completing the sentence rather than describing a separate unnamed event.

Exodus 6:29
the Lord spoke to Moses, saying, “I am the Lord. Speak to Pharaoh king of Egypt all that I say to you.”
  Exodus 6:11 — Same-chapter renewed speaking instruction: The command to speak God's words to Pharaoh returns to the earlier royal-speaking instruction, resuming that commission after the genealogical section.
  Exodus 7:2 — Word-transmission commission developed: The next divine speech specifies Moses transmitting all commanded words and Aaron speaking to Pharaoh, elaborating the communication instructed here without merging the brothers' roles.

Exodus 6:30
And Moses said before the Lord, “Behold, I am weak of voice; how then shall Pharaoh listen to me?”
  Exodus 6:12 — Same-chapter objection resumed after genealogy: Moses again doubts Pharaoh will listen because of his own speech, repeating the earlier objection with weak-of-voice wording rather than replacing it with the earlier without-eloquence expression.
  Exodus 4:10 — Earlier voice-and-tongue objection: Moses previously describes himself as weak of voice and slow of tongue; the renewed objection returns to his own speech assessment without introducing a medical diagnosis or another edition's expression.

Exodus 7:1
And the Lord said to Moses, “Behold, I have made you a god to Pharaoh; and Aaron your brother shall be your prophet.
  Exodus 4:15-16 — Delegated speaking roles with distinct audiences: The earlier commission makes Aaron Moses' mouth before the people and places Moses in matters pertaining to God; the present speech calls Moses a god to Pharaoh and Aaron his prophet, retaining both the different audience and supplied formulations.
  Exodus 7:2 — Same-chapter role description and practical instruction: The next verse specifies the commanded words passing through Moses and Aaron to Pharaoh, explaining the particular speaking arrangement behind the role descriptions given here.

Exodus 7:2
You shall speak to him all that I command you; and Aaron your brother shall speak to Pharaoh, that he may let the sons of Israel go out of his land.
  Exodus 4:15 — Earlier word-transmission instruction: God previously tells Moses to put the commanded words in Aaron's mouth; this renewed instruction specifies Aaron's speaking to Pharaoh, continuing the transmission while distinguishing the audiences.
  Exodus 5:1 — Earlier joint petition and specified roles: The first royal audience names both Moses and Aaron delivering the petition; the present commission specifies their word-transmission roles without claiming that the earlier scene independently allocated each quoted sentence to one brother.

Exodus 7:3
I will harden Pharaoh’s heart and multiply My signs and My wonders in the land of Egypt.
  Exodus 4:21 — Earlier explicit hardening announcement: God's earlier mission speech already declares that He will harden Pharaoh's heart; the present announcement repeats that divine agency and adds the multiplication of signs and wonders.
  Exodus 9:12 — Announcement and later explicit agency: The later narrator expressly says the Lord hardened Pharaoh's heart, providing a particular later instance corresponding to the agency God announces here without retroactively assigning an unstated agent to every intervening heart notice.
  Romans 9:17-18 — Later theological discussion of Pharaoh and hardening: Paul invokes Scripture concerning Pharaoh and concludes that God hardens whom He wills, providing a later theological use of the hardening theme without claiming that he quotes this particular Exodus verse or resolving every narrative agency statement.

Exodus 7:4
Pharaoh will not listen to you. I will lay My hand on Egypt and bring out My people, the sons of Israel, from the land of Egypt with My power and great vengeance.
  Exodus 3:19-20 — Earlier resistance-and-deliverance sequence: God earlier predicts Pharaoh's refusal and the wonders preceding release; the present speech repeats resistance and God's intervention to bring Israel out, preserving its supplied power-and-vengeance language.
  Exodus 12:31-37 — Announced bringing out and actual departure: The later sequence records Pharaoh's release order and Israel's departure, supplying the narrated outcome of bringing the people out after royal resistance.

Exodus 7:5
All the Egyptians shall know that I am the Lord when I stretch out My hand over Egypt and bring the sons of Israel out from among them.”
  Exodus 5:2 — Royal ignorance and promised recognition: Pharaoh's earlier statement that he does not know the Lord contrasts with God's announced purpose that the Egyptians will know Him through intervention, distinguishing the king's speech from the wider promised audience.
  Exodus 14:17-18 — Knowledge purpose repeated at the sea: God later again joins intervention against Pharaoh and his forces with the Egyptians' knowing the Lord, repeating the stated recognition purpose in a subsequent stage of deliverance.

Exodus 7:6
Moses and Aaron did as the Lord commanded them; so they did.
  Exodus 7:9-10 — Same-chapter obedience summary and particular action: The following staff command and Aaron's action expressly repeat obedience to the Lord's command, giving a specific narrated instance of the obedience summarized here.

Exodus 7:7
Moses was eighty years old and his brother Aaron eighty-three when he spoke to Pharaoh.
  Acts 7:23-30 — Moses' age and later chronological recollection: Stephen recounts Moses' visit at forty and a further forty years before the bush appearance, providing a later chronology to compare with Moses' eighty years before Pharaoh without independently dating every intervening event.
  Numbers 33:38-39 — Aaron's earlier age and later death notice: Aaron is eighty-three at the royal approach, while Numbers gives one hundred twenty-three at his death in the fortieth departure year, joining two explicit age notices in the same life.

Exodus 7:8
The Lord spoke to Moses and Aaron, saying,
  Exodus 7:9 — Same-chapter divine instruction and its content: The next verse gives the conditional instruction for the sign Pharaoh may request, supplying the content of the speech introduced to Moses and Aaron here.

Exodus 7:9
“If Pharaoh speaks to you, saying, ‘Give us a sign or a wonder,’ you shall say to Aaron your brother, ‘Take the staff, and cast it upon the ground before Pharaoh and before his attendants, and it shall become a dragon.’”
  Exodus 7:10 — Same-chapter command and enacted sign: Aaron throws the staff before Pharaoh and the attendants and it becomes a dragon, carrying out the particular sign God directs Moses to commission.
  Exodus 4:3-4 — Distinct staff-transformation scenes: Moses' earlier staff becomes a serpent and is restored, whereas the present instruction concerns Aaron's staff becoming a dragon before Pharaoh. The related signs retain their different performers, setting and creature wording.

Exodus 7:10
Moses and Aaron went before Pharaoh and his attendants and did as the Lord commanded. Aaron threw his staff before Pharaoh and his attendants, and it became a dragon.
  Exodus 7:9 — Same-chapter announced sign carried out: The narrator expressly relates Aaron's dragon-staff action to the Lord's preceding command, preserving Aaron as the named performer before Pharaoh.
  Psalms 104:26-27 — Later recollection of the commissioned sign-workers: The psalm names God's sending Moses and Aaron and placing His signs in Egypt, providing a later summary of the mission whose royal sign is enacted here.

Exodus 7:11
Pharaoh summoned the wise men of Egypt and the sorcerers. The Egyptian enchanters did the same by their sorceries.
  Exodus 7:22 — Same-chapter repeated sorcery and resistance pattern: The enchanters later imitate the blood transformation as they imitate the staff sign here, repeating their counteraction in a different wonder scene without calling the two signs identical.
  2 Timothy 3:8-9 — Later allusion to opposition to Moses: The later letter names Jannes and Jambres as opponents of Moses and compares them with its own opponents. Exodus describes Egyptian wise men, sorcerers and enchanters without naming them, so the later names are not inserted as independently verified identities for every participant here.

Exodus 7:12
Each threw down his staff, and they became dragons. But Aaron’s staff swallowed their staffs.
  Exodus 7:10 — Same-chapter initial sign and ensuing superiority: Aaron's staff first becomes a dragon and then swallows the other staffs after their transformations, connecting the original sign with the distinguishing outcome of the confrontation.
  Exodus 8:14-15 — Later limit of the enchanters' counteractions: Aaron's staff swallows the rival staffs despite their transformations; the enchanters later cannot produce gnats and speak of God's finger, giving another specific limit to their opposition without denying that the earlier text reports their transformations.

Exodus 7:13
Pharaoh’s heart grew strong, and he did not listen to them, as the Lord had said to them.
  Exodus 7:3 — Same-chapter prediction and narrated resistance: God's hardening announcement is followed by the narrator's grew-strong wording and Pharaoh's refusal to listen as foretold. The connection preserves the different expressions without supplying an agent not named in this particular notice.
  Exodus 7:22 — Same-chapter repeated resistance with different heart wording: After the blood sign Pharaoh's heart grows hard and he does not listen, repeating the present non-listening response while retaining the difference between grew strong and grew hard.
  Exodus 8:28 — Heart descriptions with different explicit agency: The present notice says Pharaoh's heart grew strong without naming an agent; the later notice expressly says Pharaoh made his heart heavy and refused release. The different wording and explicit agency are retained without inserting the later actor into this earlier sentence.

Exodus 7:14
The Lord said to Moses, “Pharaoh’s heart has become heavy, so that he will not let the people go.
  Exodus 7:13 — Same-chapter divine assessment after narrated refusal: The narrator describes Pharaoh's heart as growing strong and his refusal to listen; God's following speech describes the heart as heavy and connects it with non-release, retaining the different speaker and wording.
  Exodus 5:2 — Earlier refusal and later divine assessment: Pharaoh has already declared that he will not let Israel go; God now describes the persistent non-release through the heavy-heart assessment, supplying a divine statement about the resistance previously expressed by the king.

Exodus 7:15
Go to Pharaoh in the morning; behold, he goes out to the water. And you shall stand to meet him upon the bank of the river, and take in your hand the staff which was turned into a serpent.
  Exodus 4:17 — Earlier serpent-staff equipment recalled: Moses is told to take the staff turned into a serpent, recalling the earlier instruction to carry that sign-bearing staff without replacing serpent with the dragon term used in Aaron's royal sign.
  Exodus 7:20 — Same-chapter appointed river encounter and narrated action: The command locates Moses' meeting with Pharaoh at the river; the later strike occurs before Pharaoh and his attendants, with both brothers named and the singular he retained rather than independently resolved.

Exodus 7:16
And you shall say to him, ‘The Lord, the God of the Hebrews, has sent me to you, saying, “Let My people go, that they may serve Me in the wilderness.” And behold, until now you have not listened.
  Exodus 5:1-3 — Release-and-worship petition repeated: The first royal petition already joins the Lord, the God of the Hebrews, release and wilderness worship; the river message repeats those elements and now expressly notes Pharaoh's continued refusal to listen.
  Exodus 7:26 — Same-chapter release refrain before successive signs: The later frog announcement again begins with Let-My-people-go so they may serve, repeating the release-and-worship demand before another announced plague.

Exodus 7:17
Thus says the Lord: “By this you shall know that I am the Lord: behold, I strike with the staff in My hand upon the water in the river, and it shall be turned to blood.
  Exodus 7:20-21 — Same-chapter announced blood sign and narrated result: The river becomes blood and the fish die after the brothers' commanded action, supplying the announced transformation while distinguishing God's I-strike declaration from the human-action narration.
  Psalms 104:29 — Later recollection of the blood judgment: The psalm recalls God turning the waters into blood and killing their fish, corresponding to the announced river judgment and its effects narrated in this episode.

Exodus 7:18
The fish in the river will die, the river will stink, and the Egyptians will be unable to drink its water.”’”
  Exodus 7:21-24 — Same-chapter predicted loss of drinking water and effects: The narrator records dead fish, the river's stench and inability to drink, then Egyptians digging for water, giving the concrete consequences God predicts here.
  Psalms 77:44 — Later recollection of unusable waters: The psalm recounts rivers turned to blood and waters that could not be drunk, corresponding to the loss of drinking water predicted here while retaining its additional rain-waters wording as the psalm's own description.

Exodus 7:19

Exodus 21:12

Exodus 21:17

Exodus 21:18

Exodus 21:21

Exodus 21:22

Exodus 21:23

Exodus 21:24

  Deuteronomy 19:21 — Retaliation formula in a different judicial case: Deuteronomy repeats life, eye, tooth, hand and foot equivalents in its false-witness legislation. The shared formula is retained alongside that different legal setting rather than made a repetition of the pregnancy case.

Exodus 21:25
burn for burn, wound for wound, bruise for bruise.

Exodus 21:26
